A practical pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Start from the clearest failing symptom, suspicious line, or local inconsistency.
- Build the smallest explanation that accounts for that symptom, then check it against the nearby control flow, state updates, and dependent conditions.
- Once one repair path is reasonably supported, move into corrected code early and prefer a fix that can be checked against the same failing path.
- After the edit, rerun that path or the most relevant edge case.
- Then do one small coupled-region scan: a directly connected guard, index update, return path, or call site that could still break the same fix.
- If the recheck is still off, refine the same local region before opening a distant new theory.
- Once the failing path and its directly coupled region look consistent, finalize the code.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- staying in diagnosis mode for too long without testing the next plausible edit;
- moving from a local repair into a fresh branch of speculative causes when the current path has not been rechecked yet;
- turning a focused fix into a broad rewrite unless the surrounding code clearly forces it.

class Solution:
    def sumSubarrayMins(self, arr: List[int]) -> int:
        stack = []
        res = 0
        
        for i, num in enumerate(arr):

                cur = stack.pop()

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def sumSubarrayMins(self, arr: List[int]) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        stack = []
        res = 0
        n = len(arr)

        for i, num in enumerate(arr):
            while stack and arr[stack[-1]] > num:
                cur = stack.pop()
                left = stack[-1] if stack else -1
                res += arr[cur] * (cur - left) * (i - cur)
            stack.append(i)

        while stack:
            cur = stack.pop()
            left = stack[-1] if stack else -1
            res += arr[cur] * (cur - left) * (n - cur)

        return res % MOD

Need maybe consider if using `>` with final processing and empty stack handling is enough to pass LeetCode 907. Yes.

Let's recall LeetCode 907 official solution often uses two arrays prevSmaller and nextSmaller. Monotonic stack one-pass with sentinel is common:
```
class Solution:
    def sumSubarrayMins(self, arr: List[int]) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9+7
        stack = []
        res = 0
        for i in range(len(arr)+1):
            while stack and (i == len(arr) or arr[stack[-1]] >= arr[i]):
                cur = stack.pop()
                left = stack[-1] if stack else -1
                right = i
                res += arr[cur] * (cur-left) * (right-cur)
            stack.append(i)
        return res % MOD
```
But note if i == len(arr), arr[i] invalid. Need condition. This uses >= and sentinel. Another version:
```

Need maybe output code with `MOD = 10**9 + 7` at class level? Could be:
```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def sumSubarrayMins(self, arr: List[int]) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        stack = []
        res = 0
        n = len(arr)

        for i, num in enumerate(arr):
            while stack and arr[stack[-1]] > num:
                cur = stack.pop()
                left = stack[-1] if stack else -1
                res += arr[cur] * (cur - left) * (i - cur)
            stack.append(i)

        while stack:
            cur = stack.pop()
            left = stack[-1] if stack else -1
            res += arr[cur] * (cur - left) * (n - cur)

        return res % MOD
```
This is fixed.

Let's consider if the code should be:
```
class Solution:
    def sumSubarrayMins(self, arr: List[int]) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        stack = []
        res = 0
        for i in range(len(arr) + 1):
            while stack and (i == len(arr) or arr[stack[-1]] > arr[i]):
                cur = stack.pop()
                left = stack[-1] if stack else -1
                right = i
                res += arr[cur] * (cur - left) * (right - cur)
            if i < len(arr):
                stack.append(i)
        return res % MOD
```
This is another common one-pass with sentinel. But our separate final loop is simpler. Need ensure no bug with `arr[i]` when i==n. We avoid.

    def sumSubarrayMins(self, arr: List[int]) -> int:
        stack = []
        res = 0

Let's think about if there is a possible issue with "res" being an int and modulo at end. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a bug with "left = stack[-1] if stack else -1" in final loop after popping an element. Suppose stack is empty, left=-1. But what if the popped element is not the first element? Can stack become empty before popping the first element? In final loop, stack contains remaining elements in increasing index order. We pop from the end. The first element (smallest index) is at the bottom, so it will be popped last. Thus stack becomes empty only when popping the first element. So left=-1 only for first element. Good. In while loop, stack can become empty after popping the bottom element, which is the first element in the current monotonic sequence. That's correct.

Potential issue: If there are multiple elements in stack with equal values, final loop pops later equal first, left=previous equal. Then pops previous equal, left=previous or -1. Good.

Let's maybe think about if there is a bug with "arr[stack[-1]] > num" if current num is equal to top and there is a smaller element later. The later equal remains in stack until smaller later. When smaller later pops it, its left boundary is previous equal. Good. We tested.

Potential issue: If arr values are all equal, algorithm counts n + (n-1)+...+1 = n(n+1)/2 subarrays, each min value. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "left = stack[-1] if stack else -1" in while loop after popping cur. Suppose stack is not empty but stack[-1] is an index whose value is greater than arr[cur]? We argued impossible due to invariant. Let's prove invariant: After each iteration, stack values are nondecreasing. Initially empty. When processing num, we pop all values > num, so remaining top <= num. Then push num, so stack values remain nondecreasing. Thus before popping cur, stack values nondecreasing. Since cur is top, previous top <= arr[cur]. Good. So left boundary value <= arr[cur]. Good.

Potential issue: If stack is empty after popping, left=-1. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "left = stack[-1] if stack else -1" in while loop after popping cur. Suppose stack is not empty but stack[-1] is an index whose value is greater than arr[cur]? We argued impossible due to invariant. Let's prove invariant: After each iteration, stack values are nondecreasing. Initially empty. When processing num, we pop all values > num, so remaining top <= num. Then push num, so stack values remain nondecreasing. Thus before popping cur, stack values nondecreasing. Since cur is top, previous top <= arr[cur]. Good. So left boundary value <= arr[cur]. Good.

Suppose stack is not empty but stack[-1] is an index whose value is greater than arr[cur]? We argued impossible due to invariant. Let's prove invariant: After each iteration, stack values are nondecreasing. Initially empty. When processing num, we pop all values > num, so remaining top <= num. Then push num, so stack values remain nondecreasing. Thus before popping cur, stack values nondecreasing. Since cur is top, previous top <= arr[cur]. Good. So left boundary value <= arr[cur]. Good.

Then push num, so stack values remain nondecreasing. Thus before popping cur, stack values nondecreasing. Since cur is top, previous top <= arr[cur]. Good. So left boundary value <= arr[cur]. Good.

Suppose stack is not empty but stack[-1] is an index whose value is greater than arr[cur]? We argued impossible due to invariant. Let's prove invariant: After each iteration, stack values are nondecreasing. Initially empty. When processing num, we pop all values > num, so remaining top <= num. Then push num, so stack values remain nondecreasing. Thus before popping cur, stack values nondecreasing. Since cur is top, previous top <= arr[cur]. Good. So left boundary value <= arr[cur]. Good.

Potential issue: If stack is empty after popping, left=-1. Good.

Suppose stack is not empty but stack[-1] is an index whose value is greater than arr[cur]? We argued impossible due to invariant. Let's prove invariant: After each iteration, stack values are nondecreasing. Initially empty. When processing num, we pop all values > num, so remaining top <= num. Then push num, so stack values remain nondecreasing. Thus before popping cur, stack values nondecreasing. Since cur is top, previous top <= arr[cur]. Good. So left boundary value <= arr[cur]. Good.

Let's prove invariant: After each iteration, stack values are nondecreasing. Initially empty. When processing num, we pop all values > num, so remaining top <= num. Then push num, so stack values remain nondecreasing. Thus before popping cur, stack values nondecreasing. Since cur is top, previous top <= arr[cur]. Good. So left boundary value <= arr[cur]. Good.

Suppose stack is not empty but stack[-1] is an index whose value is greater than arr[cur]? We argued impossible due to invariant. Let's prove invariant: After each iteration, stack values are nondecreasing. Initially empty. When processing num, we pop all values > num, so remaining top <= num. Then push num, so stack values remain nondecreasing. Thus before popping cur, stack values nondecreasing. Since cur is top, previous top <= arr[cur]. Good. So left boundary value <= arr[cur]. Good.

Potential issue: If stack is empty after popping, left=-1. Good.

Suppose stack is not empty but stack[-1] is an index whose value is greater than arr[cur]? We argued impossible due to invariant. Let's prove invariant: After each iteration, stack values are nondecreasing. Initially empty. When processing num, we pop all values > num, so remaining top <= num. Then push num, so stack values remain nondecreasing. Thus before popping cur, stack values nondecreasing. Since cur is top, previous top <= arr[cur]. Good. So left boundary value <= arr[cur]. Good.

Let's prove invariant: After each iteration, stack values are nondecreasing. Initially empty. When processing num, we pop all values > num, so remaining top <= num. Then push num, so stack values remain nondecreasing. Thus before popping cur, stack values nondecreasing. Since cur is top, previous top <= arr[cur]. Good. So left boundary value <= arr[cur]. Good.

=== RAW ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def sumSubarrayMins(self, arr: List[int]) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        stack = []
        res = 0
        n = len(arr)

        for i, num in enumerate(arr):
            while stack and arr[stack[-1]] > num:
                cur = stack.pop()
                left = stack[-1] if stack else -1
                res += arr[cur] * (cur - left) * (i - cur)
            stack.append(i)

        while stack:
            cur = stack.pop()
            left = stack[-1] if stack else -1
            res += arr[cur] * (cur - left) * (n - cur)

        return res % MOD
```

=== FIXED CODE ===
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def sumSubarrayMins(self, arr: List[int]) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        stack = []
        res = 0
        n = len(arr)

        for i, num in enumerate(arr):
            while stack and arr[stack[-1]] > num:
                cur = stack.pop()
                left = stack[-1] if stack else -1
                res += arr[cur] * (cur - left) * (i - cur)
            stack.append(i)

        while stack:
            cur = stack.pop()
            left = stack[-1] if stack else -1
            res += arr[cur] * (cur - left) * (n - cur)

        return res % MOD
